# Ownership & Insider Behavior — SMPL

## 1. Ownership

SMPL (The Simply Good Foods Company) has no founder-CEO and no controlling family or promoter group. It was formed via a 2017 SPAC combination (Conyers Park Acquisition Corp + Atkins Nutritionals / NCP-ATK Holdings) and is a widely-held Nasdaq company with a single class of common stock (no dual-class or super-voting shares found anywhere in the proxy or 10-K).

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO — Joseph E. Scalzo (returned Jan 19, 2026) | Not disclosed post-return — Insufficient Data | Common (single class) | Unknown for current stake; his last on-file Form 4 balance was 132,436 shares as of Nov 13, 2024, filed while he still held equity following his Aug-2023–Aug-2024 Executive Vice Chairman role, before an ~17-month full departure and Jan-2026 return [CIQ Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls, entry 2024-11-13]. No new Form 3/4 for Scalzo is in the data pool since his return, so a current figure cannot be cited | CIQ Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls; 2026 Proxy Beneficial Ownership table (Scalzo absent — table is "as of Dec 1, 2025," before his Jan 19, 2026 return) |
| James M. Kilts (Independent Chairman) | 3.89% (3,697,024 sh, as of Dec 1, 2025) / ~4.27% per a later CIQ read (post his Apr-2026 buy) | Common (single class) | Mixed — 2,715,513 of his 3,697,024 shares (about 73% of his stake) are held indirectly through an investment limited partnership (Kilts and his spouse are co-general partners) that received its shares for no cash consideration in the Oct-2022 in-kind distribution from Conyers Park Sponsor, LLC (the original SPAC sponsor). The remaining ~1.0M shares include RSU grants plus a genuine open-market cash purchase of 80,000 shares ($991,480) on Apr 23, 2026 | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, Beneficial Ownership table, p.66, footnote (5); CIQ Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls |
| All directors & executive officers as a group (18 persons) | 8.76% (8,320,266 sh, as of Dec 1, 2025) | Common (single class) | Mixed — a combination of unvested/vested RSU and option grants, legacy Conyers Park sponsor-distribution shares (Kilts, West, Ratzan), and a small amount of recent open-market cash buying (see §2) | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, Beneficial Ownership table, p.66 |
| Largest institutional holders | BlackRock, Inc. 13.90% (proxy, Dec-31-2023 13G/A) / 14.79% (CIQ, position date Mar-31-2026); The Vanguard Group 10.97% (proxy, combined, Dec-29-2023 13G/A) / Vanguard entities ~10.55% combined (CIQ, Mar-31-2026, split across two Vanguard sub-entities); FMR LLC 6.8% (proxy, 13G filed Nov 4, 2025, as of Sep 30, 2025) | Common (single class) | N/A — institutional/index and active managers | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, Beneficial Ownership table & footnotes (2)-(4), p.66; CIQ Public Ownership Summary.rtf |
| Promoter / controlling bloc | None. The former SPAC sponsor, Conyers Park Sponsor, LLC, fully distributed its remaining shares in kind to its members in Oct-2022 and its contractual board-nomination rights (3 directors while holding ≥50% of its original stake, 1 director while holding ≥25%) lapsed the same date. A small residual "State Owned Shares" line (0.09% / 77,334 shares, likely a government pension fund's routine equity position) exists but confers no control | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "The Investor Rights Agreement," p.67-68; CIQ Public Ownership Summary.rtf |

**Total insider ownership: 8.76%** (all directors and executive officers as a group, per the SEC-filed proxy as of Dec 1, 2025) — the primary, most-current authoritative figure. A vendor cross-check (CIQ Public Ownership Summary, various position dates through Mar/Apr-2026) shows "Individuals/Insiders" at 9.30%, broadly consistent; note CIQ's own ownership-summary total sums to 108.78% of shares outstanding because, per its own footnote, "reporting requirements for holdings data is not aligned with the financials reporting of shares outstanding" — a data-quality quirk in the vendor file, not a real economic overlap, and it does not change the proxy's 8.76% figure.

This 8.76% is above what a typical widely-held, non-founder US packaged-food issuer shows, driven almost entirely by one person (Kilts) whose large stake is mostly a costless legacy distribution from the company's 2017 SPAC origin rather than cash paid at market prices. Strip Kilts out, and the remaining 17 directors/officers hold roughly 4.6M shares (~4.9%) — a more ordinary level of executive/director skin in the game, itself still a mix of grants and modest cash purchases.

**Net insider activity: net buying.** Four insiders — the independent Chairman, the CFO, an independent director, and the Chief Commercial Officer — made genuine open-market cash purchases totaling 104,946 shares (~$1.41 million) between Nov-2025 and May-2026. The only clear open-market sale in the window is a departing accounting officer's routine $114,053 disposition. Kilts's and Daley's buys both post-date the Apr-9-2026 guidance miss, meaning insiders bought after bad news was already public, not ahead of it — a conduct-positive pattern, not a red flag. West's much larger 348,000-share "Other Disposition" is flagged separately above because its type and missing price make it impossible to classify as a discretionary market sale; it is not counted in the net-buying tally, and it deserves a follow-up (see §4A).

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|

No pledging disclosed. SMPL's Insider Trading Policy contains an outright Anti-Hedging and Pledging Policy that bans officers, directors, and all other employees from pledging any company securities as collateral for a loan, holding company securities in a margin account, engaging in short sales, or entering hedging/derivative or monetization transactions with respect to the stock: "We do not allow our management or directors to pledge our stock to secure loans or other obligations" [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Anti-Hedging and Pledging Policy," p.5 (summary) and detailed policy text at p.~26 / p.~31]. No Schedule 13D/13G, Form 4, or proxy disclosure in the pool shows any pledge by an officer or director. This cross-checks cleanly with `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, which computed 0% of the insider group's holding pledged against the 50% disqualifier threshold — not triggered.

Separately, subsidiary equity interests are pledged as loan collateral under the company's corporate Credit Agreement [FY2025 10-K, Debt note] — this is ordinary secured corporate borrowing by the company itself, not promoter/insider share pledging, and is a balance-sheet matter outside this agent's scope.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | None. One class of common stock, one vote per share, throughout the proxy and 10-K | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement; FY2025 10-K |
| Controlled-company status | Not a controlled company. More than 90% of directors are independent, the Chairman is independent, and no single holder controls a majority of the vote (largest holder BlackRock at 13.90–14.79%, passive/institutional) | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Governance Practices," p.4 |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | None currently. Conyers Park Sponsor, LLC (the original 2017 SPAC sponsor) held contractual rights to nominate up to 3 directors while it held ≥50% of its original stake, and 1 director while it held ≥25%. Those rights lapsed effective Oct 13, 2022, when the sponsor made an in-kind pro-rata distribution of its remaining shares to its members (including entities controlled by Kilts, West, and Ratzan) and assigned its nomination rights to those three individuals collectively; the proxy provides no evidence that assignment remains contractually operative today, and none of the three currently controls anywhere near a blocking stake individually (Kilts 3.89%, West 2.10% pre-May-2026 disposition, Ratzan 2.16%) | 2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "The Investor Rights Agreement," p.67-68 |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | Not applicable — no government control, no parent company that could steer economics elsewhere, and no sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate structure. SMPL is a single-business, widely-held consumer-nutrition company with a dispersed institutional and modest-insider cap table | Inference from the ownership table above, Sections 1 and 4 |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6).** None of the three structural triggers applies. SMPL is not government-controlled, is not a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, and is not a sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate — it is a single-category consumer-nutrition operator whose largest holders are diversified index/active managers (BlackRock, Vanguard, FMR) and a modest, mostly-legacy insider stake. RF-OWN-004 is **not triggered**. This is a control-structure positive: no minority-holder conflict is embedded in the ownership design itself. The company's governance risk, to the extent it exists, sits in execution and capital-allocation history (see modules 01/02), not in ownership structure.

Bealer's and Clawson's buys (Nov-2025) pre-date that miss by roughly five months, well outside a pattern of "buying ahead of good news." No insider sale in the 12-month window precedes a subsequently disclosed miss; Matthews's Nov-2025 sale was a departing employee's routine disposition | CIQ Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls; `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` (FQ2 FY2026 guidance miss) |

| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | None found in the pool | CIQ Key Developments.rtf |
| Institutional (13F-equivalent) holding trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Mixed, not a sharp exit. Between Dec-31-2025 and Mar-31-2026, "The Vanguard Group, Inc." shows a decrease to 0 shares (–10,631,429) while two Vanguard sub-entities (Vanguard Portfolio Management, LLC and Vanguard Capital Management, LLC) appear as new top buyers (5,563,881 and 3,771,064 shares respectively) — this pattern is most consistent with an internal Vanguard reporting reclassification between its own management entities rather than a real reduction in Vanguard's aggregate SMPL exposure, since the combined Vanguard total (~10.55%) is close to the prior 10.97% figure. Separately, FMR LLC reduced its position by 2,656,699 shares (to 2,819,231), Boston Trust Walden National Association reduced by 1,847,699 shares (to 1,787,765), and activist fund D. E. Shaw & Co. reduced by 1,244,213 shares (to 376,229, 0.43% — its stake was never material). These reductions cluster in the same Dec-2025–Mar-2026 window as the CEO transition (Jan-2026) and the FQ2 guidance miss (Apr-2026 disclosure, which would reflect a slightly later quarter's worth of institutional repositioning) — worth monitoring, but no single holder's reduction is large enough on its own (relative to total shares outstanding) to call a "sharp" high-quality-holder exit | CIQ Public Ownership Summary.rtf, "Ownership Activity" and "Top Buyers and Sellers" sections |

No market-conduct red flag is triggered: the disclosed insider buying followed bad news rather than preceding it, and no unusual price/volume or exchange-inquiry event is documented in the pool. **Follow-up required:** confirm the character of David J. West's May-2026 348,000-share "Other Disposition" (gift/trust transfer vs. an undisclosed-price market sale) and obtain Joseph Scalzo's post-return Form 3/4 ownership figure once filed.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Insiders own a modest 8.76% of SMPL, and most of that is concentrated in one person — Chairman James Kilts — whose stake is roughly three-quarters a costless legacy distribution from the company's 2017 SPAC formation rather than cash paid at market prices; strip him out and the rest of the executive/director group holds under 5%. Set against that thin baseline, the last 12 months show a clear, if modest, positive signal: the Chairman, the CFO, an independent director, and the Chief Commercial Officer collectively put about $1.4 million of their own cash into the stock on the open market, with the two largest buys landing after — not before — the company's own guidance-miss disclosure, while the only comparable open-market sale was a departing accounting officer's routine $114,053 disposition. The biggest risk to minority holders is not pledging (none exists, and it is explicitly banned) or a controlling bloc (there is none — single share class, no controlled-company status, the former sponsor's board rights lapsed in 2022) — it is simply that ownership alignment here is thin: no one in management or on the board has enough personal capital at risk to make this look like an owner-operator story, and the current CEO's post-return stake is not yet disclosed at all.

# Board & Shareholder Rights — SMPL

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 11 directors (as constituted following the 2026 Annual Meeting and the Jan 28, 2026 board appointment) | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Election of Directors," p.23]; [CIQ Key Developments.rtf, entry dated 2026-01-28, "Scalzo appointed to the Board to fill a vacancy created by the departure of Geoff E. Tanner"] |
| Independent directors (%) | 10 of 11 = 90.9%. The Board affirmatively determined independent: Mses. Goolsby and Mally, and Messrs. Kilts, Daley, Montgomery, Ratzan, Ritterbush, Schena, West and White. The sole non-independent director is the sitting President & CEO (Geoff Tanner as of proxy mailing Dec 17, 2025; his board seat was vacated on his Jan 18, 2026 departure and filled by returning CEO Joseph Scalzo on Jan 28, 2026 — the same non-independent "CEO seat," so the 90.9% ratio is unchanged before and after the transition) | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Director Independence," p.10]; [CIQ Key Developments.rtf, entries 2026-01-20 and 2026-01-28] |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes — James M. Kilts (independent director since 2017) is non-executive Chairman; David J. West (independent, since 2017) is Vice Chairman; Joseph Scalzo is President & CEO and a director, not Chairman. The company states this is a discretionary practice, not a binding policy: "Our Board does not have a formal policy requiring the separation of the roles of Chief Executive Officer and Chairperson" | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Board Leadership Structure," p.11] |
| Audit / Comp / Nominating committee independence | All three fully independent. Audit: Schena (Chair, designated "audit committee financial expert"), Daley, Goolsby, Mally. Compensation: Goolsby (Chair), Montgomery, Ratzan, West, White. Nominating & Corporate Governance: Daley (Chair), Kilts, Ratzan, Ritterbush, White. (A fourth committee, Corporate Responsibility and Sustainability — Montgomery Chair, Mally, Ritterbush, White — is also 100% independent but is not one of the three Nasdaq-mandated committees) | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Meetings and Committees of the Board," p.19–22] |

**True-independence flag (not just a box-tick).** Three of the ten "independent" directors — Chairman James M. Kilts (Founding Partner, Centerview Capital Consumer), Vice Chairman David J. West (Partner, Centerview Capital Consumer), and director Brian K. Ratzan (Partner, Centerview Capital Consumer) — are current partners at the same private-equity/advisory firm. All three also trace back to the 2017 Conyers Park SPAC sponsor: in October 2022 Conyers Park Sponsor assigned its Investor Rights Agreement board-nomination rights "in whole to Messrs. Kilts, West and Ratzan, collectively," alongside a pro-rata in-kind share distribution to investment entities they control. They collectively hold ~8.15% of shares (Kilts 3.89%, West 2.10%, Ratzan 2.16% per the beneficial-ownership table). This meets Nasdaq's technical independence definition (no employment or business relationship with the company itself), but three board seats concentrated in the same outside firm — including the Chair and Vice Chair — is a real board-composition concentration the Nasdaq test does not capture [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions — The Investor Rights Agreement," p.68; "Election of Directors" bios, p.27, 30, 34; "Ownership of Simply Good Foods Common Stock," p.66].

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | ~6.6 years across the current 11-member board (Daley/Kilts/Montgomery/Ratzan/West all since 2017 = 9 years each; Ritterbush/Goolsby since 2019 = 7 years; Schena since 2021 = 5 years; Mally since 2025 = 1 year; Scalzo re-appointed Jan 2026 = <1 year as a continuing director, though he previously served as a director 2017–Jan 2025). Not a stale board on this metric, but 6 of 11 (55%) date to the 2017 founding/SPAC-combination cohort | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, director bios, p.25–35] |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | CEO Scalzo concurrently sits on two other public-company boards (Freshpet Inc. since Aug 2023, TreeHouse Foods Inc. since Apr 2022) plus a private board (Harvest Hill Beverage) while running a declared "turnaround" at SMPL — a bandwidth concern for a sitting CEO-director, not flagged by the company itself. Chairman Kilts (age 77) is one year from the company's own mandatory-retirement bylaw (directors cannot stand for re-election after their 78th birthday), creating near-term Chair-succession risk on top of the CEO-succession thinness already noted in `01_management-and-track-record`. No director sits on more than 2 other public boards — not overboarded by the common 3-5-board screen | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.27 (Kilts bio), p.10 (mandatory retirement policy), CIQ Key Developments.rtf entry 2026-01-19/20 (Scalzo's Freshpet/TreeHouse board seats)] |
| Recent refreshment | Romitha S. Mally (Founder, The Mally Collective; ex-Goldman Sachs/JPMorgan/Greenhill/UBS consumer investment banker) joined the Board at the January 2025 Annual Meeting — the only genuinely new independent director in the last several years. Nomi P. Ghez did not stand for re-election in Jan 2025 (director compensation table shows a partial-year payout). Geoff Tanner's board seat (the CEO seat) turned over in Jan 2026 per the leadership change already scored in `01` | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Director Compensation Table" and footnote (2), p.17–18] |

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

| RPT Type | Amount | % of Revenue | % of PAT | % of Net Worth | Arm's Length? | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Sales to / purchases from related parties | None disclosed | — | — | — | N/A — no dollar-denominated related-party sales/purchases exist in the proxy or the 10-K's Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions," p.68] |
| Loans / advances / guarantees to related parties | None disclosed | — | — | — | N/A | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.68] |
| Royalty / brand / management fees to promoter group | None — no promoter/founder group exists (SPAC-formed, widely held) | — | — | — | N/A | [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan §1, item 3] |
| Rent / other payments to promoter entities | None disclosed | — | — | — | N/A | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.68] |
| Outstanding receivables from related parties | None disclosed | — | — | — | N/A | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.68] |
| Legacy SPAC-sponsor board-nomination right | Non-monetary — a governance right, not a cash transaction: the Investor Rights Agreement's board-nomination right (originally Conyers Park Sponsor's, tied to holding ≥25%/≥50% of its July 2017 share stake) was assigned in October 2022 "in whole to Messrs. Kilts, West and Ratzan, collectively." The proxy does not clarify whether the underlying 25%/50%-of-original-stake ownership threshold is still met by the three directors' current ~8.15% combined holding, so whether this nomination right remains legally live today is not established in the pool | N/A | N/A | N/A | Disclosed but ambiguous — see Section 1 true-independence flag | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "The Investor Rights Agreement," p.68] |
| Indemnity agreements with directors/officers | Standard-form indemnification and expense-advancement agreements with every director and executive officer | — | — | — | Standard practice, not a value-leakage channel | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.68] |

**Read.** No material related-party transactions are disclosed — the proxy's related-person-transactions section contains only the legacy SPAC-sponsor registration/nomination-rights agreement and boilerplate indemnity agreements, both reviewed under a documented Related Party Transactions Policy administered by the Audit Committee (fairness-of-terms, business-purpose, independence-impairment, and conflict-of-interest factors are all named review criteria) [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Review of Related Person Transactions," p.11]. This is consistent with `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`'s finding that related-party transactions are far below the 25%-of-revenue/expense disqualifier threshold (computed at ~0%). The company's Anti-Hedging and Pledging Policy separately bans directors and officers from pledging shares as loan collateral, holding shares in margin accounts, short-selling, or hedging via derivatives — a clean, disclosed no-leakage stance [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Anti-Hedging and Pledging Policy," p.15]. There is no evidence of economic value leaking to a promoter or group — because there is no promoter or group. The one governance-texture item worth tracking is the unresolved status of the Kilts/West/Ratzan nomination right (above), which is a control-concentration question, not a cash-leakage one.

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | No formally adopted plan disclosed | No shareholder-rights plan ("poison pill") is disclosed as adopted. The board does, however, hold blank-check authority to issue preferred stock with board-set terms (including voting rights) without stockholder approval — functionally a pill-in-waiting the board could deploy against a hostile bid | [Annual Report on Form 10-K_2025, "Anti-takeover provisions...could impair a takeover attempt," Risk Factors] |
| Classified / staggered board | No | The Certificate of Incorporation provides that "all directors are elected for one-year terms" — the entire board stands for election annually, not staggered into classes | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Board of Directors," p.10] |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No | Single class of common stock, one share/one vote; confirmed on the 10-K cover page and by the beneficial-ownership table (no super-voting class) | [Annual Report on Form 10-K_2025, cover page]; [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan §1, item 2] |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | Plurality, with a majority-vote resignation overlay | Directors are elected by plurality of votes cast (no cumulative voting). A separate Majority Vote Director Resignation Policy requires any nominee who receives more "withheld" votes than "for" votes to tender a resignation, which the independent directors then accept or reject within 90 days — this policy does not apply in contested elections | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Majority Vote Director Resignation Policy," p.24] |
| Shareholder ability to call meetings / act by written consent | No on both | The charter and bylaws prohibit stockholder action by written consent (forcing all stockholder action to a meeting) and prohibit stockholders from calling a special meeting — only the Chairman, the CEO, or a board majority may call one. Directors may also be removed only "for cause" and only by a majority of the voting power of all outstanding shares (not just shares present and voting) — a materially higher bar than removal-without-cause-by-simple-majority-present regimes | [Annual Report on Form 10-K_2025, "Anti-takeover provisions in our amended and restated certificate of incorporation..." Risk Factors] |

**Read.** The capital-structure and election mechanics are shareholder-friendly on their face (single class, no staggered board, annual elections, a real majority-vote-resignation policy for uncontested elections). But the Delaware charter/bylaw stack layered on top — no written consent, no shareholder right to call a special meeting, board-controlled vacancy-filling, blank-check preferred, for-cause-only removal at a majority-of-outstanding (not majority-present) threshold, and advance-notice bylaws — is a standard but real anti-takeover and entrenchment toolkit that the 10-K itself flags as capable of "impair[ing] a takeover attempt" and "mak[ing] the removal of management more difficult." None of this crosses into a classified board or dual-class stock, but it is not a "clean" shareholder-rights charter either.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| AGM votes against (remuneration / RPT / director / auditor resolutions) | FY2024 say-on-pay (voted at the January 2025 Annual Meeting) was approved by 97.3% of votes cast — low opposition, no red flag. The outcome of the January 28, 2026 Annual Meeting (the meeting covered by this proxy, held after the CEO transition) is **not confirmed in this data pool** — the proxy statement itself predates the meeting (mailed Dec 17, 2025) and no post-meeting 8-K/voting-results filing is present | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Fiscal Year 2024 Say-on-Pay Voting Results," p.53 and Proposal 5, p.82] |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations (ISS / Glass Lewis / IiAS) | Not disclosed in this data pool — no ISS or Glass Lewis report is present | — |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | Not separately disclosed; the 97.3% FY2024 say-on-pay approval implies broad institutional support at that vote, but current-cycle (2026) opposition data is unavailable (see above) | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.53] |
| Dilution history (preferential allotment, warrants, QIP, ESOP pool) | The 2025 Incentive Plan (Proposal 3, approved by the Board on Oct 16, 2025, subject to stockholder vote) requests an incremental 2,250,000 shares — 2.36% of the 94,999,174 shares outstanding as of the Dec 1, 2025 record date — on top of 4,098,405 shares carried over from the prior plan (combined pool ~6.35M shares, ~6.7% of shares outstanding). No "evergreen" auto-refresh provision. Historical simple burn rate: FY2023 0.67%, FY2024 0.45%, FY2025 0.60% — modest and stated as in line with compensation peers. No preferential allotments, warrants, or QIP-style placements are disclosed | [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, "Approval of The Simply Good Foods Company Incentive Plan," p.72–73] |

No High-materiality AGM-opposition red flag is present on the data available (97.3% is well inside the Green band), but the module cannot confirm the most recent (2026, post-shakeup) vote — this is a genuine, dated gap, not a governance concern in itself. RF-SHR-001 (high votes against) is **not triggered** on available evidence; RF-SHR-002 (controversial dilution) is **not triggered** — the incentive-plan request is modest, disclosed, and burn-rate-justified.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

The board is legally independent by the numbers — 10 of 11 directors, all three Nasdaq-mandated committees fully independent, single-class stock, annual (not staggered) elections, and a real majority-vote-resignation policy — and there is no promoter, founder, or government owner to misalign it (RF-OWN-004 does not apply: SMPL is widely held, largest holder Blackrock at 13.9% is a passive index manager, and combined insider ownership is 8.76% [2026 Annual Meeting Proxy Statement, p.66]). Related-party leakage is essentially absent: no disclosed related-party sales, loans, or fees, and a clean anti-hedging/anti-pledging policy. Against that, two specific findings keep this from "strong": first, three of the ten "independent" directors — including the Chairman and Vice Chairman — are current partners at the same outside firm (Centerview Capital Consumer) and jointly inherited the SPAC sponsor's board-nomination rights, a true-independence concentration Nasdaq's technical test does not catch; second, the Delaware charter/bylaw stack (no written consent, no shareholder right to call a special meeting, for-cause-only removal at a majority-of-all-outstanding-shares bar, blank-check preferred) gives the board — not shareholders — control of the escalation path if a future dispute arises, exactly the tools a captured or complacent board could use against an activist or an unwanted suitor. The single biggest entrenchment risk to minority holders is that combination: a board whose Chair/Vice-Chair/one-more-seat cluster around one advisory firm sits behind a charter that shareholders cannot bypass without going through that same board. **Verdict: adequate** — not "strong," because the independence-in-substance and entrenchment findings above are real and specific, not "weak/entrenched," because there is no controlling owner, no material RPT leakage, and shareholder support (97.3% say-on-pay) has been high on the one vote this pool can confirm.

## Board & Shareholder Rights Score

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Board independence | 16 | 20 | 90.9% independent by Nasdaq test (05-001); chair/CEO split is real but discretionary, not policy-locked (05-002) |
| True independence / tenure / overboarding | 12 | 20 | Real refreshment and non-stale average tenure (05-005, 05-008), offset by the three-director Centerview Capital Consumer interlock at Chair/Vice-Chair level (05-004), CEO-director overboarding during a turnaround (05-006), and Chair-succession risk one year out (05-007) |
| Committee quality | 14 | 15 | Audit/Comp/Nominating all 100% independent, financial expert on Audit, regular meeting cadence (05-003) |
| RPT and group-leakage protection | 18 | 20 | No material dollar RPTs, documented Audit-Committee-run policy, clean anti-hedging/pledging ban (05-009, 05-010, 05-012); docked for the unresolved status of the legacy SPAC-sponsor nomination right (05-011) |
| Shareholder voting rights | 9 | 15 | Single class, annual elections, no staggered board (05-014, 05-015), but no written consent, no shareholder right to call a special meeting, and a for-cause/majority-of-outstanding removal bar (05-016, 05-017) — a real, not cosmetic, limitation on minority power between annual meetings |
| AGM opposition / minority protection | 7 | 10 | Strong FY2024 say-on-pay support (97.3%, 05-019), but the most recent (2026, post-shakeup) AGM outcome is unconfirmed in this pool (05-020) and no proxy-advisor data exists (05-021) |
| **Total** | **76** | **100** | Strong-band overall (61-80 per CLAUDE.md §12), driven down from "very strong" by the true-independence interlock and the shareholder-rights charter provisions, not by any RPT leakage or ownership-control problem |

No Partial-Data score cap from `MODULE_RULES.md` applies — the proxy statement discloses full board composition, committee membership and independence, and related-party transactions, so board disclosure is not "unavailable." No hard disqualifier is flagged by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`, and RF-OWN-004 does not apply, so no additional cap tier is triggered.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — SMPL

**Data basis:** Two VERBATIM earnings-call transcripts are in the pool — FQ2 FY2026 (Apr 9, 2026) and FQ3 FY2026 (Jul 9, 2026), both S&P Global Market Intelligence transcripts with full prepared remarks and Q&A [The Simply Good Foods Company, Q2 2026 Earnings Call, Apr 09, 2026.rtf; The Simply Good Foods Company, Q3 2026 Earnings Call, Jul 09, 2026.rtf]. No shareholder letter or prior-CEO-era transcript is in the pool, so the tone/candor read is limited to these two quarters — both under the CURRENT (returning) CEO, Joe Scalzo — and does not cover how the PRIOR management team (Tanner) communicated its own FQ2 FY26 guidance miss before departing (he had already left by the time that miss was reported). `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` is available and used directly for the non-GAAP cross-check; no cap applies on that account. No partial-data cap applies to the tone/Q&A/owning-the-miss components — both are assessed off verbatim transcripts, not proxies.

## 1. Owning the Misses

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| FQ2 FY26 net-sales guidance missed by -5.1% to -6.1% (guided $343.5M–$347.1M, actual $326.0M) | CFO Bealer opened with "we are disappointed with our Q2 performance as our retail takeaway slowed significantly compared to Q1"; CEO Scalzo opened the entire call with "we are not pleased with our performance. We've experienced executional challenges" and closed with "we are not satisfied with our current performance." Both executives then walked through brand-by-brand root causes (Quest bar softness, OWYN product-quality issue, Atkins distribution losses) without attributing the miss to macro or one-off excuses | **Owned plainly** — blunt, unhedged language, given at the top of the call rather than buried, with a granular root-cause breakdown by brand | [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks (CEO Scalzo, CFO Bealer)] |
| OWYN acquisition (~$280.4M, Jun-2024) — implicit growth-platform promise, 71% of purchase price impaired by FQ3 FY26 | CEO: "we did not meet our own expectations with the integration of the brand into our company. As a result, we lost some important brand expertise, our marketplace execution was poor and our brand performance fell well short of our plans" [Q2 FY26 transcript]. On the Q3 call he reiterated the failure was "integration and execution issues rather than a lack of consumer demand," explicitly not blaming the category or the deal thesis | **Owned plainly** — a rare instance of a CEO naming his own organization's execution failure on a $280M deal in specific terms, not hedged behind "challenging environment" language | [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks; Q3 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks] |
| Prior leadership's strategic drift (implicit indictment of the Tanner-era choices that preceded the CEO change) | Scalzo, without naming Tanner: "Strategy shifted, priorities were not always clear and execution did not consistently meet the standard required to compete." He also named specific prior decisions as mistakes: cutting Atkins marketing "negatively affected net sales," over-extending distribution ahead of consumption, "the pacing of the investment was unfortunate and poorly timed" | **Owned, diplomatically framed** — substantively candid about what went wrong, but attributed to "we" / "the company" rather than naming the departed CEO by name (normal governance practice, not evasion of substance) | [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks and Q&A] |
| FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA guidance narrowly missed (-0.6% below guided floor) | Routine-magnitude miss; not separately addressed as a standalone "miss" narrative on either FY2026 call — folded into the broader FY2025 discussion in the 10-K MD&A | Not a material miss — too small to test candor on | [Annual Report on Form 10-K_2025; `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4, finding 01-007] |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | "Integration expense" (OWYN) has been added back to Adjusted EBITDA in every reported period since the FY2024 acquisition — $0.6M FY2024, $20.9M FY2025, $5.2M FQ3 FY2026 — despite being labelled non-recurring each time. "Restructuring" was zero FY2021–FY2023, reappeared at $18.3M FY2024, $23.1M FY2025, and again at $13.5M in FQ3 FY2026 alone. Stock-based compensation ($15.3M FY2025, $18.4M FY2024, $5.6M FQ3 FY2026) is excluded from Adjusted EBITDA every single period, with no exception | earnings/06 §4 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | Total non-GAAP adjustments were 56.3% of GAAP EBITDA in FY2025 ($100.2M of $177.9M) and 17.7% in FY2024 ($40.4M of $228.8M) — both well above the 15% materiality threshold this module treats as a warning line. In FQ3 FY2026 the adjustment ($101.1M) exceeded GAAP EBITDA in absolute dollar terms because GAAP EBITDA was negative that quarter | earnings/06 §4, §7, §9 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Yes, materially. Adjusted EBITDA has excluded a $391.9M cumulative goodwill/brand impairment recognized across three of the last four fiscal quarters (FQ4 FY25 $60.9M; FQ2 FY26 $249.0M; FQ3 FY26 $82.0M) — all stemming from the same two acquisitions (OWYN, and the older Atkins carrying value). None of the three disclosures individually is a concealment (each is quantified and reconciled in the filings), but the repetition means the company's primary "clean" KPI structurally cannot show investors that a real, cash-relevant pattern of value destruction from prior M&A has now recurred three times in a year | earnings/06 §1, §10; cross-ref `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4, finding 01-006 |

`RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)`

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Management discloses brand-level KPIs that actually drive the thesis — household penetration percentage, consumption/retail-takeaway growth, and buy rate — separately for Quest, Atkins, and OWYN on both calls, and the same KPI set is reported consistently quarter over quarter rather than swapped out when a brand looks bad (Atkins household penetration and its 22–24% consumption decline were disclosed in full on both calls, not omitted) [Q2/Q3 FY26 transcripts]. Two disclosure gaps partially offset this: the 10-K and 10-Qs do not break out maintenance versus growth capex despite a step-change in total capex (0.4% of revenue FY2024 → 1.4% FY2025 → 2.0% TTM), leaving investors to guess how much of that increase is discretionary [earnings/06 §1]; and the FY26 capital-expenditure guidance figure disagreed between the verbatim Q3 transcript ($25M–$30M) and the same-dated Capital IQ Guidance tab ($20M–$25M) — likely a vendor-parsing discrepancy rather than a company inconsistency, but unresolved in the pool [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §2].

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

The worst recent period by results was FQ2 FY26 (the quarter of the material guidance miss, the $249.0M impairment, and the CEO/CFO/Controller leadership turnover, landing within weeks of each other). On that call the tone was candid rather than promotional: both executives opened with unhedged admissions of disappointment before any positive framing, and the CEO gave granular, brand-specific root-cause diagnosis (competitive bar pressure, an OWYN product-quality defect, over-extended distribution) rather than generic "macro headwinds" language. Q&A on both calls was answered directly and at length — analysts pressed repeatedly on timing, and Scalzo responded with explicit uncertainty rather than deflection ("my crystal ball is kind of cloudy," "the when, it's going to come in stages... my crystal ball is not that good, frankly") instead of a false-precision turnaround date. No question went unanswered or was redirected without substance in either transcript. The one recurring stylistic pattern worth flagging is that blunt near-term admissions are consistently followed by extended "why I remain confident" passages built on category attractiveness and brand equity — legitimate context, but a repeated rhetorical structure that softens the landing of each admission.

## 5. Candor Read

Management's communication under the current CEO is genuinely candid on the two dimensions that matter most — owning misses and answering questions directly — with blunt, specific, unhedged language used for both the FQ2 guidance miss and the OWYN acquisition failure, and no evasive non-answers detected across two full verbatim Q&A sessions. The strongest evidence for trust is the OWYN admission itself ("our marketplace execution was poor and our brand performance fell well short of our plans") — a specific, self-critical statement rare in CPG earnings calls, made without deflecting to competitive or macro excuses. The strongest evidence against full trust is structural rather than tonal: Adjusted EBITDA, the company's primary "clean" KPI, has excluded a $391.9M impairment pattern recurring across three of the last four quarters, and integration/restructuring costs have been labelled "one-off" while recurring every period for over two years — a genuine non-GAAP discipline problem that sits alongside, not instead of, the candid verbal tone. No unresolved adverse integrity signal was routed from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (§3 of that report found all eight hard disqualifiers clean and flagged nothing for this agent to weigh), so this read is not compounded by an unaddressed integrity concern per CLAUDE.md §24 Filter 1. On balance: **mixed** — candid tone and direct Q&A behavior, undercut by aggressive non-GAAP framing that structurally hides a real, recurring pattern of M&A-driven value destruction from the headline metric investors are pointed to.

`RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)`
